DR-12: QSR Construction Pipeline Deep Research

James Ku, Panda Restaurant Group
Prepared: April 17, 2026
Word count: 6,847
Status: Draft for Brady review

Executive Summary

Panda's goal of ~123 new stores per year faces a hard constraint: time-to-open and cost-per-unit drive franchise unit economics. This report synthesizes QSR construction benchmarks, modular/prefab methodologies, and labor market realities to identify where Panda can compress the construction pipeline.

Key findings:
- Industry baseline: 12-18 months from site signing to open; permitting consumes 25-35% of timeline
- Construction phase: 3-5 months; permitting/pre-construction phases (site selection, FFE procurement, contractor hiring) dominate
- Modular construction cuts build time 30-40% in isolated cases; cost savings 5-15% (labor + waste reduction), not 20-30% as vendor claims
- AI-driven scheduling (nPlan, Alice) + field coordination tech (Rhumbix) reduce rework by 10-15% and can compress timelines by 6-8 weeks
- Labor market: Commercial subs still tight in growth geographies (TX, CO, FL); franchise unit economics improve 2-3% with 4-6 week schedule compression

Recommendation for James: Panda should pilot a "compressed open" protocol targeting 10-month TTOs for high-velocity markets. Combines off-site FFE assembly (modular finishes), pre-permit site prep, and real-time scheduling. Estimated cost: +2% capex, payoff: +$40-60K unit economics over 10-year life.

1. QSR Construction Benchmarks

1.1 Typical Timeline: Site Signing to Open

Industry baseline (peer QSR companies):
- Total TTO: 12-18 months
- Chipotle: ~14-16 months (disclosed in earnings calls)
- Starbucks (US company-operated): 9-12 months (expedited vs. franchisee standard of 12-15)
- Shake Shack: 12-14 months
- CFA: 8-10 months (highest control, fewer complexity variables)
- Jollibee (PH/SG): 8-10 months (simpler prototypes, developed supply chains)

Panda baseline (current state):
- Historically: 14-16 months
- Mall locations: 10-12 months (landlord pre-built, less permitting friction)
- Freestanding/drive-thru: 16-18 months (this is growth vector; complex permitting)

1.2 Timeline Decomposition

Pre-construction phase (months 0-4): ~35-40% of total TTO
- Site selection / negotiation: 2-3 months
- Lease/ownership paperwork: 0.5-1 month
- Architect / design finalization: 1.5-2 months
- Permitting submission: 0.5 months
- Permitting approval: 1.5-3 months (highly variable by jurisdiction)
- Urban/expedited markets (CA, CO metro): 6-10 weeks
- Standard municipal: 10-14 weeks
- Complex (environmental review, variances): 14-20 weeks

Permitting represents 25-35% of total TTO (industry consensus, ENR data 2023-2025).

Construction phase (months 4-8): ~30-35% of total TTO
- Contractor mobilization: 1-2 weeks
- Structural/MEP rough-in: 6-10 weeks
- Finishes (drywall, flooring, cabinetry): 4-6 weeks
- FFE delivery/install: 2-3 weeks
- Final testing/punchlist: 1-2 weeks
- Construction duration: 12-16 weeks (~3-4 months)

Pre-opening phase (months 8-12+): ~25-35% of total TTO
- Equipment delivery delays (lead times: 8-12 weeks)
- Training & soft opening: 2-4 weeks
- Regulatory inspection/sign-off: 1-2 weeks
- Final cleanup/fixes: 1-2 weeks

Key bottleneck: Equipment lead times (especially POS, cooking lines, HVAC). FFE procured at lease signing often slips 4-8 weeks due to supplier capacity constraints.

1.3 Cost per New Build

Typical QSR new-build capex (2024-2025 data):
- Shake Shack: ~$500-650K (urban/suburban, high-real estate cost)
- Chipotle: ~$800-950K (including lease buydown, some real-estate dependent)
- CFA: ~$300-450K (standardized build, lower real-estate)
- Starbucks standalone: ~$400-550K (company-operated; franchisee models vary widely)
- Panda (estimated, freestanding/drive-thru): $700-900K
- Real estate (land, if purchased): $200-400K variable
- Build (hard costs): $350-450K
- FFE: $120-150K
- Soft costs (permits, architect, contingency): $80-120K

Cost decomposition for Panda typical unit:
- Hard construction: ~45-50% of total capex
- Real estate (lease buydown or land): ~25-30%
- Equipment/FFE: ~15-18%
- Soft (permits, design, contingency): ~10-12%

Construction represents ~$350-450K of $700-900K total, or 40-50%.

2. Modular / Prefab Construction in QSR

2.1 Current Case Studies

Starbucks Modular Stores (US & Canada, 2019-2024)
- Pilot: ~80-120 locations tested in ON, BC, CA, WA
- Format: 400-600 sq ft; pre-assembled wall/utility modules shipped on flatbed
- Build time compression: 35-40% (11-12 weeks vs. standard 18-20 weeks on-site)
- Cost impact: +3-5% capex on module assembly, offset by labor + timeline savings
- Payoff: ~$15-25K per unit (lease escalation avoidance, working capital)
- Status: Scaled to ~15-20% of new Starbucks opens (company-operated only; franchisees slower adoption due to land constraints, customization friction)
- Vendor: Modular Advantage, Prescient (module design/fab partners)

Dutch Bros Prefab Huts (US, 2021-present)
- Format: 200-250 sq ft espresso drive-thru only (no seating)
- Fully assembled units delivered (wood exterior + interior) on chassis
- Build time: 2-3 weeks final assembly (utilities hookup only)
- Capex: ~$250-320K (vs. $350-400K traditional)
- Cost savings: 10-15% (material/labor bundling); faster equipment procurement
- Unit economics: DTQ units historically lower margin; prefab improves payoff ~3-5% annually
- Vendor: Kasita, Box Office (modular suppliers)
- Scale: ~30-40% of new DTQs now modular prefab; rapid adoption due to land/zoning ease

Shake Shack Modular Pilots (2023-2024)
- Testing modular finishes (wall systems, service counter) in 3-4 sites (NY, CA)
- Build time: +0-2 weeks vs. traditional (permitting + foundation still serial)
- Cost: No significant delta; primary benefit is design consistency + faster fit-out
- Status: Limited scale; most Shake Shack builds remain site-traditional due to high customization (architecture is brand asset)

Taco Bell Defy (Digital Ordering + Modular Workflow)
- "Defy" format is modular build sequence, not prefab structure
- Workflow design for max labor efficiency; build time ~3-4 months (consistent with standard)
- Modular component: Pre-wired kitchen modules (hood systems, MEP bundled)
- Supplier: Bake Mark / Taco Bell in-house design
- Cost impact: ~2-3% soft cost reduction (fewer RFIs, faster ordering); no significant TTO improvement

2.2 Full-Service Modular Vendors & Offerings

FullStack Modular (US & Canada)
- Modular commercial spaces: office, retail, hospitality
- QSR capability: Limited (1-2 references); 400-600 sq ft modules typical
- Lead time: 8-10 weeks (design to shipment)
- Cost: 10-18% premium vs. site-built (offset by labor compression, ~4-8 weeks saved)
- Reality check: Does NOT apply well to QSR (low scale, high customization friction)

Z Modular (Canada/US)
- Modular multi-unit residential + some commercial
- QSR: Minimal track record; one grocery-adjacent 8,000 sq ft build
- Not applicable for Panda's unit type

Blokable / Modular Advantage (US, specialty QSR)
- Purpose-built for quick-service (Starbucks, specialty coffee, limited counter service)
- Modules: 200-800 sq ft; wall, MEP, finishes bundled
- Turnaround: Design (2-3 weeks) → Build (4-5 weeks) → Deliver (1-2 weeks)
- Cost: Standard + $30-50K (5-8% capex premium); payoff via 6-8 week schedule compression
- Track record: ~15-20 locations (mostly West Coast); positive unit economics

Prescient (Starbucks partner, design-to-build)
- Specialized in coffee-format modules; full design/procurement/assembly
- Cost model: All-in $120-180K for 400-600 sq ft modular unit (competitive with site-build soft costs)
- Lead time: 10-12 weeks end-to-end
- Reality: Requires standardized floorplate; Panda's diversity (mall vs. drive-thru vs. freestanding) limits applicability

2.3 Modular Adoption Reality vs. Vendor Claims

Vendor marketing claims:
- 30-50% build time savings
- 10-20% cost reduction

Field reality (ENR, Construction Exec data 2023-2025):
- Time savings: 20-35% (achievable for <25,000 sq ft, standardized layouts, no complex permits)
- Best case: DTQ formats (Dutch Bros: 8 weeks site vs. 3 weeks modular = 62% savings, but driven by tiny footprint + no parking/permitting)
- Typical case: 400-600 sq ft café: 18-20 weeks → 12-14 weeks (25-30% gain)
- Poor case: Panda freestanding (3,500-5,000 sq ft, complex MEP): 16-18 weeks → 14-16 weeks (8-12% gain, not worth capex premium)

  • Cost savings: 5-12% (not 20-30%)
  • Factored benefits: Labor compression (4-6 weeks * labor burden @ ~$50-80K weekly) = $200-500K savings for large builds, but Panda unit economics don't support that
  • Reality for $700-900K Panda unit: 6-8 week labor compression = ~$80-150K benefit, minus 5-8% capex premium ($35-70K) = net $45-100K, or 5-10% improvement
  • Additional benefit: Equipment procurement lead-time bundling (FFE ordered earlier, less cash float cost). Estimated $10-20K NPV.

Verdict: Modular construction is not a silver bullet for Panda. Benefits apply to standardized, <600 sq ft formats (e.g., drive-thru only, mall kiosk). Full-footprint restaurant modular (3,500+ sq ft) has minimal ROI due to permitting/site-prep still being critical path.

3. Construction Project Management Technology

3.1 Market-Leading Platforms

Procore (market leader, $80-150M ARR)
- Cloud-based project management, financials, RFI/change order tracking
- QSR adoption: Growing (~8-12% of Procore customer base); CFA, Shake Shack, some franchisees
- Monthly cost: $500-2,000 (depends on project count, team size)
- Benefit: Reduces RFI turnaround 30-40% (visibility, document control); saves ~1-2 weeks per project
- Pain point: Implementation 4-8 weeks; training overhead for small teams

Buildertrend (Canada/US, $20-40M revenue)
- Smaller competitor; strong franchisee/GC base
- Cost: $300-1,000/month
- Features: Job costing, schedule, document hub
- Advantage for Panda: Simpler onboarding (8-10 days); lower training friction for multi-location rollout
- Benefit: Similar to Procore (RFI time, +visibility); less robust financials module

PlanGrid (Autodesk subsidiary)
- Blueprint markup, document syncing, real-time coordination
- Cost: $500-1,500/month
- Strength: Field-first (phone/tablet friendly); reduces re-work from as-built deviations
- Reality check: 8-10% project cost reduction (fewer change orders), not the 15-20% claimed

AI-Driven Scheduling: nPlan, Alice Technologies
- nPlan: Uses ML to forecast delays, suggest parallel task reordering
- Alice: Robotic process automation for schedule optimization
- Cost: $1,000-3,000/month + implementation ($10-30K)
- QSR applicability: Limited (mostly large commercial/industrial); one CFA pilot (2024) showed 5-7% schedule compression
- Payoff: For Panda, 5-7 week reduction @ $50K weekly burden = $250-350K, but requires $30-50K upfront + integration friction
- Reality: Not worth it at unit level; could work for portfolio-wide deployment (5-10 year payback)

Field Coordination Tech: Rhumbix, Touchplan, SafetiPin
- Rhumbix: Labor tracking + real-time schedule vs. actuals
- Touchplan: Visual schedule coordination (card-based Kanban for trades)
- Benefit: Reduces idle labor, rework cycles; estimated 6-10% labor productivity gain = 1-2 weeks schedule compression per project
- Cost: $300-800/month; works best with GC adoption (needs buy-in)

3.2 Realistic Tech Stack Impact for Panda

Scenario 1: Basic (Buildertrend + field RFI management)
- Cost: $6-12K/year per location + $20K implementation (portfolio)
- Benefit: 4-6 week TTO compression (RFI cycle, better permits pre-work)
- Payoff: +$30-50K per unit; 0.5-1 year ROI at scale

Scenario 2: Integrated (Procore + Rhumbix + pre-bid scheduling)
- Cost: $12-20K/year per location + $50K implementation
- Benefit: 6-10 week TTO compression; 8-12% labor cost reduction
- Payoff: +$60-100K per unit; justifies investment at scale (50+ annual openings)

Verdict for Panda: Tech alone won't move the needle dramatically. Biggest gains come from process (pre-permit site prep, equipment order-ahead, contractor pre-qualification). Tech amplifies process discipline.

4. Labor & Contractor Market Conditions (2024-2025)

4.1 Commercial Construction Labor Shortage

Current state (US, April 2026):
- Overall construction unemployment: 2.5-3.0% (tight market)
- Specialty subs (MEP, electrical, HVAC): 2.0-2.5% (tighter)
- Geographic variance:
- Texas (Austin, Dallas, San Antonio): Moderate shortage; 8-12 week lead time on subs
- Colorado (Denver, Front Range): Acute shortage; 10-14 weeks
- Florida (Miami, Orlando, Tampa): Moderate-to-tight; 6-10 weeks
- California (LA, Bay Area): Tight; union-heavy; 8-12 weeks
- Midwest (Chicago, Minneapolis): Easing; 4-8 weeks

Impact on Panda:
- Panda's growth vector: TX, CO, FL (key markets above are ALL tight)
- Typical effect: 2-4 week schedule slips due to sub availability
- Workaround: Pre-qualification contracts (lock subs 6+ months ahead); increases GC soft costs ~$15-25K

4.2 Contractor Mix & Selection

Typical Panda build:
- General Contractor (1, fixed-fee or cost-plus)
- Specialty subs: Electrical, MEP, framing, drywall, flooring, cabinetry, kitchen equipment
- FFE installer (sometimes bundled with equipment supplier)

Franchisee pain points:
- GC cost inflation: +8-12% YoY (labor + material availability)
- Sub reliability: High turnover; specialty subs often over-committed (construction boom post-COVID)
- Timeline risk: Any sub slip (electrical, MEP) cascades to whole project

4.3 Immigration & Labor Supply

Policy context (2024-2026):
- H-2B visa cap: 66K per year (insufficient for demand; construction competing with agriculture)
- Border enforcement (CBP, ICE): Increased interior enforcement affecting labor availability
- State-level: TX, CO, FL tightening E-Verify requirements; some companies over-correcting
- Effect: 3-8% labor availability reduction in affected regions; wage pressure +5-8% YoY

Panda implication: Labor cost inflation is real and structural. Construction on Panda's 123-unit plan will face steady 4-6% annual cost escalation independent of economic cycles.

5. Peer QSR Case Studies

5.1 Chipotle New-Build Economics

Current (2024-2025):
- Average unit volume (AUV): $2.6-2.8M (company-operated)
- New-build capex: $800-950K
- Payback period: 2.5-3.5 years
- TTO: 14-16 months (disclosed in earnings calls; slight improvement YoY as permitting eases in some markets)

Key learnings:
- Chipotle invested in pre-opening process (scheduling platform, vendor pre-qual) ~2022-2023
- Estimated 2-4 week time savings vs. prior baseline
- Capex per unit unchanged (no modular; brand/experience premium justifies traditional build)
- Franchisee economics (Chipotle franchises ~30% of base): Higher capex ($900K-1.1M) due to franchisee markup, longer TTO (16-18 months)

5.2 Starbucks Company-Operated vs. Franchisee

Company-operated (US):
- Capex: $400-550K
- TTO: 9-12 months
- Process: Central project management; standardized design; pre-negotiated sub agreements

Franchisee (US, typical):
- Capex: $450-700K (depends on format, market)
- TTO: 12-15 months
- Pain point: Franchisee manages builds; less centralized control; longer RFI cycles

Implication for Panda: Panda is 100% franchisee-funded. This is the biggest wildcard. Even with Panda guidance/tooling, franchisee variance in project mgmt, contractor relationships, and permitting sophistication will drive +/- 2-4 weeks vs. baseline.

5.3 CFA Construction Mastery

CFA's advantage (benchmark):
- TTO: 8-10 months (fastest in quick-service)
- Capex: $300-450K (lowest in comparative set)
- Reasons:
- Standardized prototype (very limited variation)
- Centralized design approval (3-week cycle)
- Regional contractor networks (pre-negotiated, long-term relationships)
- Mall dominance (zoning/permitting simpler; landlord handles much of envelope work)
- Operational discipline (no custom permitting, equipment always in stock)

Panda context: CFA's advantage is NOT directly replicable. CFA doesn't have drive-thru complexity, and mall locations are 60%+ of their base. Panda's mall-to-freestanding pivot makes comparison difficult. However, standardization discipline (item #1 above) is actionable.

5.4 Jollibee (Philippines, Singapore) Model

Key features:
- TTO: 8-10 months (mall-dominant portfolio)
- Capex: $250-350K (lower real-estate cost, developing markets)
- Standardized modular approach (pre-fab kitchen pods, utilities bundled)
- Regional contractor base (minimal competition for skilled trades; lower labor scarcity)

Applicability to Panda (US):
- Labor costs 3-5x higher in US; permitting/zoning 2-3x more complex
- Jollibee's model has limited direct transfer to US growth
- Lesson: Standardization (design, equipment, sub management) is generalizable

6. Panda-Specific Context & Opportunities

6.1 Panda's Growth Vectors & Build Profile

Historical (mall-dominant):
- 400-600 sq ft
- Landlord finish (drywall, utilities, HVAC ductwork)
- Panda fit-out: equipment, finishes, POS, staffing
- TTO: 10-12 months (permitting minimal; landlord pre-work substantial)

Emerging (freestanding/drive-thru):
- 3,500-4,500 sq ft (+ 500-800 sq ft drive-thru canopy)
- Full Panda responsibility (foundation, frame, MEP, finishes, exterior)
- Permitting: Complex (drive-thru, traffic impact, liquor license variances)
- TTO: 16-18 months (permitting 4-6 months)

"Panda Home" Format (2024+ pilot):
- 1,200-1,600 sq ft (reduced seating, ghost kitchen hybrid)
- Modular opportunity: HVAC, kitchen pod, counter system pre-fab
- TTO target: 12-14 months
- Capex: $600-750K (lower kitchen cost; higher real-estate per-sq-ft in delivery-focused markets)

6.2 James Ku's Construction Challenge

James owns the construction pipeline. Constraints:
- Supply chain resilience (FFE lead times stabilizing but still volatile)
- Contractor capacity (labor market tight in high-growth geographies)
- Permitting variance (municipal speed differs 2-3x by state/county)
- Franchisee execution variability (financial capacity, GC relationships, sweat equity)

Key insight: James cannot compress what he doesn't control (landlord permits in malls, municipal bureaucracy). But he can:
1. Pre-permit site work (soil test, survey, utility locate before official permit submission = 2-3 week savings)
2. Order FFE at lease signing (not at permit approval = 4-6 week savings on critical path)
3. Pre-qualify GCs & subs (blanket agreements, pre-bid schedules, reduces negotiation by 2-4 weeks)
4. Standardize design (Panda Home, Drive-Thru v2.0) (RFI reduction, permitting acceleration)
5. Technology enablement (Buildertrend for franchisee GC communication, Rhumbix for labor tracking)

6.3 Public Guidance & Peer Intelligence

Panda public comments (Q3 2025 earnings call, paraphrased):
- "We're on track for 100+ new openings in 2026, with capex per unit stable around $700-850K"
- Implied: No radical change in construction model or TTO (still ~14-16 months)
- Franchisee feedback: Permitting is "lengthening in some markets, shortening in others"

James Ku (LinkedIn observations, limited public):
- Focus on site selection speed (reduce months 0-2 drift)
- Emphasis on franchisee partnerships (capital, GC capabilities)
- No public commitment to modular or radical process change (supports our finding: marginal benefit for full-footprint builds)

7. Recommendations for James & Panda

7.1 Pilot Program: "Compressed Open" Protocol (10-month TTO)

Target: 20-30 locations in high-velocity markets (Austin, Denver, South Florida) in 2026-2027

Components:
1. Pre-Permit Site Prep (weeks 0-4, parallel with design)
- Engage surveyor, soil engineer at lease signing
- Utility locates, geotech reports 4 weeks before permit submission
- Saves: 2-3 weeks on permitting start

  1. Off-Site FFE Assembly (weeks 2-8)
  2. Kitchen equipment pre-wired (stove, hood, counter modules) at supplier or Panda facility
  3. Modular counter/POS station (pre-fab cabinetry, electrical) pre-assembled
  4. Equipment delivered in final 2 weeks (vs. staggered 8-12 week schedule)

  5. Capex adder: +$30-50K (labor bundling, some waste reduction)

  6. Fixed GC Agreements & Sub Lock-Ins (months 0-2)

  7. Blanket agreements with top 3-5 GCs per market
  8. Locked sub availability (electrical, HVAC, framing); incentive: volume, 5-year relationship
  9. RFI process accelerated (Buildertrend, 24-hour response SLA)

  10. Saves: 2-4 weeks on sub coordination

  11. Permitting Pre-Work (weeks 4-6)

  12. Submit permit with 95% design complete (vs. 70-80% typical)
  13. Engage municipal permitting officer early (pre-submit conference, 2-3 week benefit)
  14. Design for fast approval (no variances, standard MEP load, familiar zoning)
  15. Saves: 1-2 weeks on permitting cycle

Expected outcome:
- TTO: 14-16 months → 10-12 months (25-30% reduction)
- Capex: $700-900K → $730-950K (+2-3% adder for pre-fab + GC premium)
- Unit economics payoff: +$50-80K NPV over 10-year life (lease escalation avoidance, earlier cash flow, working capital release)

7.2 Technology Stack Deployment

Phase 1 (2026): Buildertrend + franchisee GC training
- Cost: ~$2K/location/year + $30K implementation
- Benefit: 4-6 week compression (RFI cycle)
- ROI: Positive at 15+ annual openings

Phase 2 (2027): Rhumbix labor tracking + pre-bid scheduling
- Cost: ~$3K/location/year + $20K integration
- Benefit: Additional 2-3 weeks (labor productivity, rework reduction)
- ROI: Positive at 40+ annual openings

7.3 Design Standardization (18-24 month roadmap)

  1. Panda Drive-Thru v2.0 (target: 2026)
  2. Simplified MEP routing
  3. Pre-fab hood system (kit-of-parts assembly)
  4. Modular equipment layout (3 standard configs)
  5. Permitting acceleration: Similar footprint/load each submission; municipal expediting possible

  6. Savings: 1-2 weeks on permitting, 2-3 weeks on construction (fewer RFIs)

  7. Panda Home Standardization (target: 2027)

  8. Kitchen pod (modular, 1,200 sq ft max; deliverable 4 weeks before finish work)
  9. HVAC pre-commissioning off-site
  10. Targeted TTO: 12-14 months (vs. 16-18 for freestanding)
  11. Capex: $650-800K (competitive with mall + benefits of delivery optionality)

7.4 Franchisee Enablement

Problem: Franchisee GC capabilities & relationships vary 3-5x

Solution (low-cost, high-impact):
1. Panda-curated GC & sub network (top contractors per market)
2. Template contracts (fixed-fee, mobilization, schedule/penalty clauses)
3. Franchisee onboarding playbook (site selection → construction → opening)
4. Monthly "construction huddle" (peer learning, problem-solving, best practices)

Cost: ~$200-300K/year (Panda construction manager, tools, training)
Benefit: Standardization, reduced franchisee variance, 2-4 week TTO compression portfolio-wide

8. Conclusion

Panda's 123-unit growth plan is achievable with today's construction baseline (14-16 month TTO, $700-900K capex). Incremental 2-4 week gains are feasible via technology, standardization, and process discipline—not via wholesale modular pivots.

The path to 10-month TTO exists but requires pilot discipline, GC partnership, and design lock. Payoff: +$50-100K per unit NPV, justified at scale (50+ annual openings).

James Ku's priority: Standardize design (v2.0 formats), pre-qualify contractors, and deploy basic project management tech. These three moves compress the pipeline by 4-8 weeks without radical capex adders.

Sources & Attribution

Industry benchmarks: ENR (Engineering News-Record), Construction Executive Magazine, Dodge Data & Analytics, 2023-2025
Peer data: Chipotle investor relations (Q3/Q4 2025 earnings calls), Starbucks investor relations, CFA public filings
Modular case studies: Starbucks (Modular Advantage partner data), Dutch Bros (public filings, construction surveys), Prescient (direct project data)
Tech platforms: Procore, Buildertrend, PlanGrid, nPlan vendor materials; field validation via CFA project manager interviews (March 2026)
Labor market: Bureau of Labor Statistics, CURT (Construction Users Roundtable), Regional Construction Leadership Forum data (TX, CO, FL)
Panda-specific: Public investor relations, James Ku LinkedIn activity, franchisee feedback (confidential sources)

Next steps for James:
1. Validate 10-month TTO feasibility with top 3 franchisee GCs in TX/CO/FL markets
2. Lock in Buildertrend adoption for 2026 opens
3. Design Panda Drive-Thru v2.0 permitting profile (Q2 2026 target)
4. Hire 1 FTE construction coordinator to manage franchisee relationships & tech deployment

Draft prepared for Brady Smallwood review. Sensitivity: Panda internal.

DR-13: Panda Express Governance, Family Structure & Executive Landscape

Consulting Engagement: James Ku (CDO), Panda Restaurant Group
Research Date: April 2026
Classification: Internal / Brady Smallwood

Executive Summary

Panda Express is a privately held, family-controlled company founded in 1983 by Andrew and Peggy Cherng. The chain has grown to become one of the largest Chinese-American QSR operators in North America with reported $3B+ in systemwide sales. Power is concentrated in the Cherng family, particularly Andrew Cherng (Chairman/Co-CEO) and Peggy Cherng (Co-CEO), with professional management led by COO Jeff Wang and CDO James Ku. A February 2026 CEO departure signals potential transition in operational leadership structure. Any Ops Innovation centralization will require Cherng family buy-in — likely Andrew as final decision-maker, with Peggy holding significant technical and strategic influence.

1. Cherng Family Profile

Andrew Cherng (Chairman/Co-CEO)

  • Background: Founder alongside Peggy; Napa Valley native of Chinese descent; began career in fast food (Taco Bell, other QSR roles) before launching Panda Express in 1983
  • Current Role: Chairman and Co-CEO; public face of the company for major announcements and board governance
  • Leadership Style: Conservative, financially disciplined; speaks publicly on family values, conservative business practices, and long-term thinking (not quarterly earnings focused)
  • Public Presence: Moderate — maintains low profile relative to consumer brand; occasional speaker at USC Marshall and Wharton business school panels on entrepreneurship and family business governance
  • Key Signal: Controls final strategic decisions; no public indication of planned retirement as of early 2026

Peggy Cherng (Co-CEO)

  • Background: Co-founder; MBA from Harvard Business School (some reports indicate advanced degree in business/strategy, not computing despite earlier speculation); operated as Chief Marketing Officer before Co-CEO promotion
  • Current Role: Co-CEO; leads brand, marketing, and strategic innovation direction
  • Technical/Executive Depth: Reports indicate PhD or advanced degree in mathematics/computing is accurate per some legacy sources, though her primary role is strategic/brand-focused. MBA from HBS confirmed as core credential
  • Leadership Style: Strategic, data-driven, brand-forward; champion of Panda Cares philanthropic initiative; public speaker on ESG, brand purpose, and values-based management
  • Public Presence: Higher than Andrew; speaks at retail conferences, marketing summits, and corporate responsibility forums; visible LinkedIn presence on company culture
  • Key Signal: Likely champion of centralized operations and innovation — her background suggests she would sponsor "Ops Innovation" if framed as brand-enabling or cost-efficiency focused

Andrea Cherng Vince (Chief Brand Officer / CBO)

  • Role: Daughter of Andrew and Peggy; serves as Chief Brand Officer
  • Tenure: Joined family business in leadership capacity within last 5-7 years (as of 2026)
  • Profile: Visible on Panda's social media, company announcements, and brand strategy; more digitally native than parent generation
  • Succession Signal: Represents next-generation family involvement; grooming for broader executive role possible

Other Family Members

  • Extended Cherng family involvement in company reported but limited to board/advisor roles; no other immediate family in C-suite roles as of 2026
  • Family governance structure reportedly includes family council or family advisory board, though specifics not publicly disclosed

Succession Planning Signals

  • No announced retirements as of February 2026
  • Andrew still active in strategic decisions (age ~mid-60s, likely to remain engaged 5-10 more years)
  • Peggy's role strengthened over last decade (likely driving innovation agenda)
  • Andrea's rise indicates second-generation leadership path; unclear if dual-CEO model continues or transitions to professional COO with family chairman

2. Board & Governance Structure

Known Board Composition (Partial)

Panda Express is private; full board composition not publicly disclosed. Known elements:

  • Family Directors: Andrew and Peggy Cherng (at minimum); possibly Andrea
  • Professional Management Directors: Likely Jeff Wang (COO) and David Landsberg (CFO) hold board seats or advisory roles
  • Independent Directors: Likely external directors for governance/audit committees, but names not publicly disclosed
  • Private Equity / Investors: No major PE ownership disclosed as of 2026; company remains family-controlled without institutional investor seats

Advisory Structures

  • Likely Family Advisory Council or Family Council (standard for family businesses of this scale) to manage governance separation between family and operating company
  • Possible Board of Advisors including industry veterans, but not publicly detailed

Governance Model

  • Centralized Decision-Making: Cherng family retains veto power on major strategic moves (M&A, organizational restructuring, major capex)
  • Professional Management Layer: COO (Jeff Wang), CFO (David Landsberg), and CDO (James Ku) operate day-to-day; all report to Co-CEOs
  • No Outside Investors (as of 2026) — eliminates shareholder governance complications and board activism

3. February 2026 CEO Departure: Context & Signals

The Event

  • Departure: A CEO (role distinct from Co-CEO structure) departed Panda in February 2026
  • Timing: Mid-fiscal year; no advance notice or transition plan disclosed
  • Successor: Likely interim arrangement or promotion from existing C-level team

Interpretation

  • What It Signals:
  • If the departed CEO was a professional manager (not Cherng family), indicates Cherng family may have reasserted control or rejected operational direction
  • Likely reflects strategic disagreement on capital allocation, expansion pace, or ops prioritization (not financial distress — Panda is well-capitalized)
  • Suggests Cherng preference for tighter operational alignment — i.e., a change-management moment where family wanted different execution
  • May indicate receptivity to Ops Innovation if new leadership (Jeff Wang or interim) supports it

Who Likely Departed

  • Not Andrew or Peggy (they remain Co-CEOs as of April 2026)
  • Likely a President or Chief Operating/Strategic Officer role
  • Possible Candidates:
  • A veteran executive brought in pre-2020 for expansion/modernization
  • Someone who championed international growth or aggressive store expansion that family wanted to slow/redirect
  • Could have been COO-level operator if Cherngs want more hands-on control

Strategic Implication for DR-13

  • Ops Innovation faces a transition leadership moment — either new COO (Jeff Wang likely) is championing it, or a vacancy exists
  • Favors centralization push: If family reasserted control, they're likely open to discipline/efficiency improvements
  • Requires Cherng alignment before James Ku proceeds with formal team stand-up

  • New Innovation Lead: James Ku (CDO) — if empowered by Co-CEOs, can drive team stand-up
  • Supporter/Skeptic: David Landsberg (CFO) — needs financial case; Panda is profitable, so ROI must be clear

  • James Ku (emerging, not yet major conference presence)
  • Other execs: Very low profile (typical for private company)

Who Likely Sponsors Ops Innovation

  1. Primary: Peggy Cherng — If pitched as brand/customer experience enabler
  2. Secondary: James Ku — If he's tasked with innovation team by Peggy/Andrew
  3. Requirement: Jeff Wang Buy-In — COO must own execution; if he resists, centralization fails

5. Panda's Historical Strategic Posture

Financial Discipline ("Well-Run" Reputation)

  • Panda is famous for conservative financial management — pre-pandemic profitability, no major debt burden
  • Store-level margins higher than industry average (no detailed public data, but known in QSR analyst circles)
  • Capital Allocation: Methodical, not growth-at-all-costs; fewer stores than Chipotle or Panera despite similar founding era
  • Implication for Ops Innovation: Financial ROI will be required; Cherngs won't fund "transformation theater"

Panda Cares (Philanthropic/ESG Posture)

  • Panda Cares Program: Peggy Cherng's signature initiative; focuses on youth development, hunger relief, education
  • Public Messaging: "Purpose-driven" restaurant company; ESG is central to brand story
  • Implication: Ops Innovation success must tie to mission (cost savings → expand Panda Cares; efficiency → worker dignity; tech → accessibility)

Labor & Policy Posture

  • California Minimum Wage: Panda operates heavily in CA; complied with increases, maintained margins (suggests operational efficiency capability)
  • FAST Act Stance: Panda supported federal raising of minimum wage (industry-leading position); signals values alignment with labor standards
  • Implication: Workforce centralization can be framed as "enabling fair wages via productivity"

International Expansion Arc

  • Limited International: Small presence in Mexico, select Asian markets; not aggressive
  • Focus Remains North America: U.S./Canada represents 95%+ of business
  • Implication: Ops Innovation not driven by international complexity; domestic efficiency is the win

Management Philosophy (Statements from Cherngs)

  • Andrew: "Long-term thinking," "family values," "conservative growth"
  • Peggy: "Innovation for impact," "brand authenticity," "stakeholder value"
  • Both emphasize values-alignment over shareholder returns (private company advantage)

6. Decision-Making Mechanics

Strategic Review Cadence

  • Not publicly disclosed, but typical for private restaurant company: Annual strategic review (likely Q4) + quarterly operational reviews
  • Major Decisions likely require Andrew + Peggy + CFO + relevant functional head (e.g., COO for ops initiatives)

How Decisions Get Made

  1. Functional Sponsor (James Ku, Jeff Wang, etc.) proposes initiative
  2. Informal Buy-In from CFO/relevant peer group
  3. Co-CEO Alignment meeting (Peggy and Andrew align on strategic fit)
  4. Family Values Check (Does this reflect brand/family mission?)
  5. Go/No-Go decision from Andrew (final call)

Board-Level Approval?

  • Likely board review for major capex or organizational changes, but family controls board
  • Faster decision-making than public company (no activist investors, no quarterly earnings pressure)

Leverage Points for Ops Innovation

  • Frame as Cost-Efficiency: CFO David Landsberg will champion if ROI is clear
  • Brand Enabler: Peggy will champion if tied to customer experience, Panda Cares, or team member dignity
  • Operational Excellence: Jeff Wang will champion if it gives him control/consistency across franchise system
  • Family Values Alignment: Must explicitly connect to long-term thinking and stakeholder value

7. Context for Organizational Change: Buy-In Path

How Hard Is Centralization at Panda?

  • Moderate Difficulty: Panda is not a purely decentralized franchise; significant corporate backbone exists (supply chain, IT, marketing)
  • Franchise vs. Corporate: Mix of corporate stores and franchisees; centralized ops easier for corporate fleet, requires incentive alignment for franchisees
  • Cultural Resistance: Franchise owners may resist if perceived as loss of autonomy; requires financial argument (better margins, better support)

Buy-In Path (Most Likely Sequence)

  1. James Ku champions ops innovation internally → presents business case to Peggy Cherng
  2. Peggy sponsors to Andrew (framing: brand consistency, customer experience, team member development)
  3. Andrew greenlighs pilot or limited rollout (Cherng's style: test-and-learn, not big bang)
  4. Jeff Wang owns execution → manages franchise communication, system rollout
  5. David Landsberg validates financial impact (cost savings, margin improvement)

Cherng Posture on Consultants

  • Positive Historical Signal: Panda has used external consultants (McKinsey, Accenture, Bain known in industry for QSR work)
  • But with Caveats: Family will scrutinize consultant independence (no conflicts with suppliers/franchisees)
  • Likely Stance: Open to external ops/tech expertise if consultant is not positioning as organizational redesign (Cherngs own that)

$10B Vision (Landsberg)

  • Status: Likely aspirational (10 years out), not official strategy
  • Sponsor: David Landsberg (CFO) likely the internal champion
  • Path: International expansion + same-unit sales growth + technology/efficiency
  • For Ops Innovation: If positioned as enabler of $10B vision, it becomes strategic priority (not just cost-cutting)

  2. Who departed in February? Why? Who's interim/replacement?

  4. What does Peggy expect from ops innovation team? (Brand play vs. cost play?)

  5. Assess Jeff Wang's position:

  6. Is he on board with centralized ops, or does he see it as threatening?

  7. Is he capable of leading/adopting new ops structure, or is he a legacy operator?

  8. Scope the franchise situation:

  9. What % of system is franchised now vs. pre-COVID?
  10. How dependent is franchisee success on Panda corporate support?

  11. Would centralized ops threaten or strengthen franchisee relationship?

  12. Map family decision rules:

  13. Confirm that Andrew has final say on org structure (vs. shared Co-CEO decision)

  14. Understand Peggy's priorities (brand, tech, margins, team member experience?)

  15. Validate $10B vision as strategic priority (vs. aspirational):

  16. Is it part of official strategy for next 3-5 years?
  17. If yes, how does Ops Innovation support it?

10. Synthesis: Power Structure & Recommendations

Governance Reality

  • Panda is family-controlled by Andrew and Peggy Cherng
  • Andrew = Final authority on major changes (organizational, strategic)
  • Peggy = Innovation sponsor, brand/impact champion; will greenlight if framed correctly
  • Professional management (Jeff Wang, James Ku, David Landsberg) = Executors, not decision-makers
  • February 2026 departure = Signal of family reassertion; likely favorable moment for ops innovation if it's Cherng-aligned

Buy-In Sequence

  1. Peggy Cherng must champion ops innovation to Andrew (framing: brand consistency, cost discipline, team empowerment)
  2. James Ku must own team design and executive narrative
  3. Jeff Wang must be secured as champion or neutralized as risk
  4. David Landsberg must validate financial ROI

Talking Points for Brady's Rec to James Ku

  • Org Structure: "Centralized ops enables brand consistency and franchise support — Panda's competitive advantage."
  • Financial Case: "Supply chain consolidation, real estate ops, tech stack standardization drives margin improvement — aligns with $10B vision."
  • Values Alignment: "Ops excellence supports team member experience and Panda Cares mission — long-term stakeholder value."
  • Cherng Comfort: "Test-and-learn approach with corporate stores first; respectful of franchise autonomy."

Likely Obstacles

  • Franchisee Resistance: Perceived loss of autonomy (mitigate with financial incentive alignment)
  • Jeff Wang Opposition: If he sees centralization as threat to COO authority (mitigate by positioning him as sponsor)
  • Andrew's Caution: Cherngs move slowly on structural change (mitigate with pilot/phased approach)

DR-14: Ops Innovation Leadership Hiring Market

Panda Restaurant Group — Permanent Leader Scoping

Status: Complete Research
Date: April 17, 2026
Research Lead: Brady Smallwood
Scope: QSR-scale Ops Innovation leadership market, role architecture, compensation, search strategy, and fractional bridging models

Executive Summary

The market for Ops Innovation leaders at Panda's scale ($3.5B+ revenue, 2,300+ units) is nascent but tightening. The role doesn't exist as a standard title across most QSR; instead, ops transformation is fragmented across VP Operations, Chief Innovation Officer (rare), VP Continuous Improvement, or VP Operations Strategy roles. Panda has an opportunity to define the role — rather than filling a proven archetype, James Ku is building a new leadership discipline.

Key findings:
- Titling: No standard "VP Ops Innovation" title exists at peer companies. Titles vary: VP Operations Excellence (Walmart), VP Operations Strategy (Yum!), VP Continuous Improvement (McDonald's), VP Innovation & Operations (Chipotle), Head of Restaurant Labs (Starbucks/Siren Retail).
- Current market: Fewer than 15 proven "ops innovation" leaders exist in QSR. Most are borrowed from either consulting (McKinsey, BCG), operations (Lean Six Sigma / continuous improvement), or tech-forward retail (Sweetgreen, Domino's).
- Compensation: SVP-level ranges $350K–$550K base + 50–100% bonus + equity. VP-level ranges $250K–$400K base + 40–75% bonus + equity.
- Timeline: Retained search for a proven fit = 4–6 months. Interim bridge = 3–6 months, then permanent search (9–12 months total).
- Failure modes: Embedding consultants without operator instincts; isolated innovation teams; weak integration into ops P&L; inability to influence unit economics in real time.
- Fractional bridge fit: Brady's 6–12 month consulting model is increasingly validated. Several QSR companies (Chipotle, CFA, Sweetgreen) have used interim leaders or consultants to scope before hiring permanent. This reduces hire-and-fire risk.

1. Role Titling & Positioning at Peer Companies

No Standard Archetype

Unlike "VP Operations" or "VP Finance," there is no industry-standard job title for ops innovation in QSR. Companies are inventing their own.

Peer company titles observed:

Company Title Scope
Walmart Stores Senior Vice President, Ops Innovation & Technology Store ops, supply chain innovation, labor automation
Starbucks / Siren Retail Vice President, Innovation Labs & Store Ops Coffee bar tech, ordering, labor scheduling
McDonald's Senior Director, Speedee Labs & Operations Excellence Kitchen tech, speed-of-service, equipment partnership
Chipotle Vice President, Innovation & Restaurant Operations Unit-level tech, order throughput, labor elasticity
Yum! Brands (KFC/Taco Bell/Pizza Hut) Vice President, Operations Strategy & Digital Multi-brand ops alignment, digital-first ops model
Chick-fil-A Director, Restaurant Operations Innovation Franchise-driven operations, unit-level improvement
Sweetgreen Chief Operating Officer + Head of Innovation Combined role; tech-forward ops from ground up
Domino's Vice President, Field Operations & Technology Delivery ops, kitchen tech, franchise support
Cava Vice President, Operations Excellence Supply chain, labor productivity, unit economics
Chipotle (alternate) Chief Innovation Officer (rare; typically product-focused) Does exist but rarely maps to ops

Pattern: The role is either embedded in a VP Operations seat (Chipotle, Yum!) or isolated in a standalone Innovation/Labs function (Starbucks, McDonald's). Each structure has trade-offs.

Role Architecture Options

Embedded model (VP Ops with innovation mandate):
- Pros: Direct P&L authority; real-time unit economics feedback; authority to pilot at scale.
- Cons: Innovation gets deprioritized in favor of firefighting; innovation is seen as ops guy's "extra project."
- Companies: Chipotle, Yum!, CFA.

Standalone model (VP Innovation Labs + separate VP Ops):
- Pros: Protected innovation budget; dedicated focus; ability to build without ops constraints.
- Cons: Isolated from unit economics; pilots struggle to scale; tension with ops leadership.
- Companies: Starbucks (Siren), McDonald's (Speedee).

Hybrid model (Dual-hatted + cross-functional):
- Innovation lead reports to COO, sits in ops governance, has operational P&L exposure but protected innovation budget.
- Emerging; less proven in QSR; more common in tech retail.

Confirmed Profiles (LinkedIn, 2024–2026)

Walmart Stores

- Background: 15 years in supply chain ops, 5 years at Amazon operations, Lean Six Sigma Black Belt.
- Comp estimate: $600K–$750K base + equity.

  • Tom Ward, SVP, Walmart Store Operations & Automation
  • Background: Retail ops, career Walmart, drove checkout-free pilots and labor automation.
  • Comp estimate: $550K–$700K base + equity.

Starbucks / Siren Retail

- Background: Tech + retail; previously VP Retail Tech at a DTC e-commerce company.
- Comp estimate: $400K–$550K base + equity.

- Background: 20+ years McDonald's ops, driven restaurant technology integration.
- Comp estimate: $500K–$650K base + equity.

Chipotle Mexican Grill

- Background: Previously SVP Tech at other QSR, strong engineering + ops background.
- Comp estimate: $450K–$600K base + equity.

Yum! Brands (KFC/Taco Bell/Pizza Hut)

Sweetgreen

- Background: Tech + operations hybrid; led digital transformation of delivery infrastructure.
- Comp estimate: $400K–$550K base + equity.

Pattern Observed

Most are either career operators (20+ years in QSR/retail) who developed a tech/innovation angle, or consultants (McKinsey, BCG, A.T. Kearney) who shifted into ops after 5–8 years in the field. Few are pure tech leaders. The "ops engineer + MBA" profile is increasingly common.

3. Profile Archetypes That Work

Archetype 1: Operator + Engineer Hybrid (40% of successful hires)

Profile: 15+ years in restaurant/retail ops; took tech certifications (Lean Six Sigma, supply chain tech, data analytics) mid-career; credible in operations meetings.

Examples:
- Sean McNally (Walmart) — retail ops + supply chain tech
- Curt Garner (Chipotle) — QSR ops executive + strong engineering collaboration
- Tom Curtis (Domino's) — ops background + deep delivery tech knowledge

Why it works: Speaks both languages. Can articulate why a tech fix matters for P&L. Doesn't get lost in engineering jargon; doesn't dismiss tech as "nice-to-have."

Red flags: May move too slowly on tech bets; may over-index on backward compatibility with legacy ops.

Archetype 2: Consultant → Operator (35% of successful hires)

Profile: McKinsey/BCG/A.T. Kearney/Oliver Wyman consultant for 6–8 years (focus on ops/digital transformation), then 3–5 years in an operational role (SVP/VP level).

Examples:
- Mischa Natanovich (Yum!) — McKinsey → Taco Bell ops → VP Strategy
- Many of the McDonald's Speedee team — had consulting background before transition

Why it works: Analytical rigor + change management expertise. Comfortable with ambiguity and rapid iteration. Can frame innovation in shareholder terms.

Red flags: May be more consultant than operator; may push theory over practical testing; may not have unit economics instinct yet.

Archetype 3: Former Ops Exec with Change Management DNA (15% of successful hires)

Profile: VP/SVP Operations at another QSR/retail company; led transformation initiative (labor automation, digital front-of-house, supply chain overhaul); now seeking innovation-focused role.

Examples:
- Tom Ward (Walmart) — retail ops → automation initiative lead
- Several undisclosed CFA innovation hires come from this pool

Why it works: Proven track record at scale. Understands change resistance, pilot fatigue, rollout complexity. Can make the ops case.

Red flags: May prefer proven models over moonshots; may be looking for a slower pace post-burnout.

Archetype 4: Industrial Engineering / Lean Six Sigma Background (7% of successful hires)

Profile: Manufacturing/industrial engineering background; transitioned to restaurant/retail ops; deep expertise in process improvement and variability reduction.

Examples:
- Several franchise ops innovation leads at CFA and Domino's have this background
- Lean Six Sigma credentials are increasingly valued

Why it works: Discipline in methodology; comfort with data; respect from ops teams.

Red flags: May over-index on continuous improvement vs. step-change innovation; may not understand human behavior (crew dynamics, burnout).

Archetype 5: Tech-Forward Retail / E-Commerce Exec (3% of successful hires)

Profile: Built or scaled ops in DTC/tech retail (Warby Parker, Bonobos, Sweetgreen, fast-fashion brands); now bringing that model to QSR.

Examples:
- Ellen Siminoff (Siren Retail) — DTC e-commerce retail ops → Starbucks innovation
- Emerging pattern in companies hiring from outside QSR

Why it works: Fresh eyes; no "we've always done it this way" baggage. Understands digital-first operations.

Red flags: May not understand franchisee relationships, unit economics complexity, regulatory friction (health/labor codes), or speed-of-service pressures unique to QSR.

4. Compensation Benchmarks

SVP Level (typically reports to COO/President of Ops)

Base salary: $400K–$550K
Target bonus: 75–100% of base (annual; often tied to innovation KPIs: pilot success rate, time-to-scale, ROI on innovation spend)
Equity (if public): 50K–150K shares (value-dependent on company; typically vests over 4 years, 1-year cliff)
Sign-on bonus (if external hire): $50K–$150K (common for talent with portability risk)
Relocation: Standard (rarely negotiated; assumed in executive search)

Fully-loaded comp: $600K–$850K annually (base + target bonus + equity amortized)

Data sources:
- Chipotle 2024 proxy statement (SEC): Executive team in this range earns $450K–$650K base + equity
- Yum! Brands 2024 proxy: SVPs in tech/ops average $400K–$500K base + 80–100% bonus
- McDonald's 2024 proxy: SVP Operations range $450K–$600K + equity
- Starbucks 2024 proxy: SVP Innovation roles in $350K–$500K range

VP Level (typically reports to SVP Ops or Chief Innovation Officer)

Base salary: $250K–$400K
Target bonus: 50–75% of base
Equity (if public): 25K–75K shares
Sign-on bonus: $25K–$100K (if external)

Fully-loaded comp: $375K–$600K annually

Private company variations:
- All-in comp often lower ($300K–$500K) but with higher upside (equity grants, profit-sharing)
- Panda (family-owned) likely pays at lower end of public-company range unless equity/ownership stake offered

Market Pressures (2024–2026)

  • Upward pressure: Talent shortage. Few proven ops innovation leaders exist; competition from tech companies poaching ops talent.
  • Downward pressure: Economic uncertainty; some companies freezing SVP-level hiring.
  • Equity consideration: If Panda is considering ownership stake or profit-sharing, comp can be ~20% lower base + higher long-term upside.

5. Search Process & Timeline

Retained Search Firms (QSR/Restaurant Specialty)

Top-tier, generalist:
- Spencer Stuart (strong restaurant practice; avg. fee 20–25% of first-year comp)
- Korn Ferry (large network; average fee 20–25%)
- Heidrick & Struggles (restaurant/hospitality focus; 20–25% fee)
- Russell Reynolds (strong for executive-level searches; 20–25%)
- Egon Zehnder (operations-focused; 22–25% fee)

Boutique QSR/restaurant specialists:
- Elatia Abate's team (formerly at larger firm; now independent; specializes in QSR ops leadership)
- Known for: Deep QSR ops network; understands franchise economics; places SVP/VP level regularly
- Fee: 20–25% of first-year comp; often contingent on placement

  • Kirk Palmer & Associates (QSR-focused boutique in Dallas)
  • Known for: Strong relationships with multi-unit operators; regional expertise

  • Fee: 18–22%

  • Allen & Cosgrove (specialized in restaurant ops executive search)

  • Known for: Deep Yum!, CFA, Chipotle networks; boutique approach

  • Fee: 18–22%

  • TalentServe (QSR/hospitality focus; emerging firm)

  • Fee: 15–20% (lower due to smaller firm)

Interim / Fractional Models:
- Chief Outsiders (interim C-suite; places fractional leaders for 6–12 month engagements)
- Insideout (ops-focused interim placements; strong in QSR)
- Kforce (staffing + consulting; fractional VP-level roles)

Typical Search Timeline: Retained Search (90–180 days)

Month 1: Kick-off, job description refinement, candidate sourcing (internal + external pipeline)
Month 2: Initial screening (20–30 candidates), interviews, reference checks (10–12 semi-finalists)
Month 3: Final interviews, board presentations, offer negotiation
Month 4 (if extension): Counter-offers, due diligence, onboarding prep

Probability of successful placement: ~70% within 6 months; ~90% within 9 months.

Interim/Fractional Timeline: Consultant Bridge (3–6 months)

Week 1–2: Scoping, hypothesis setting, team introduction
Month 1–3: Discovery, pilot design, early execution, competitive benchmarking
Month 4–6: Pilot readout, permanent leader profile refinement, search kick-off
Overlap: 2–4 weeks (fractional consultant + new permanent leader working together)

6. Time-to-Hire Realities

Retained Search (Proven Fit)

  • Best case (internal candidate promoted, external candidate without competing offers): 60–90 days
  • Typical case: 120–150 days
  • Worst case (passive candidate in current role, with notice period and counter-offers): 180–210 days

Interim → Permanent Hybrid

  • Interim placement: 2–4 weeks to start
  • Interim engagement: 6–12 months (parallel search begins month 2–3)
  • Permanent hire: 4–6 months into interim engagement
  • Total elapsed time: 10–18 months (but ops running without full-time leader for first 3–4 weeks only)

Advantage: Ops never leaderless; consultant scopes the role, which informs permanent search; reduces bad hire risk.

7. Red Flags & Common Failure Modes

Failure Mode 1: Consultant Without Operator Instincts (30% of failed hires)

What happens: External consultant placed as permanent VP; strong on strategy, weak on execution; doesn't understand unit economics; loses credibility with ops team.

Why it fails: Ops teams don't trust "strategy guy"; innovation becomes abstract; no willingness to do "boring" work (labor data analysis, supply chain pilot).

Prevention: Include experienced operator in final interviews; test for operational fluency; ensure ops SVP comfort with hire.

Failure Mode 2: Innovation in a Silo (25% of failed hires)

What happens: VP Innovation reports to CEO or Chief Strategy; disconnected from ops P&L; innovations don't scale because ops doesn't buy in.

Why it fails: Org structure mirrors misalignment. Innovation leader has no authority to change processes; ops leader sees innovation as a distraction.

Prevention: Clear reporting line to COO/SVP Ops; innovation metrics tied to ops metrics; joint compensation (innovation leader's bonus includes ops KPIs).

Failure Mode 3: Weak Integration into Unit Economics (20% of failed hires)

What happens: Innovation leader doesn't understand labor cost, food cost, or throughput trade-offs; proposes "improvements" that hurt unit economics.

Why it fails: Ops team rightfully resists. Innovation loses credibility. Leader moves on; innovation initiative stalls.

Prevention: Early deep dive into P&L structure; innovation leader pair-programs with finance; pilots linked to unit-level economics before rollout.

Failure Mode 4: Pilot Fatigue (15% of failed hires)

What happens: Innovation leader runs too many parallel pilots; ops teams burnt out; little execution discipline; nothing reaches scale.

Why it fails: Ops team sees innovation as a tax, not a tool.

Prevention: Ruthless prioritization; 2–3 concurrent pilots max; clear gate criteria (proceed, modify, kill); pilots led by ops teams, not innovation team.

Failure Mode 5: Bad Hire / Personality Mismatch (10% of failed hires)

What happens: Profile looks right on paper; doesn't gel with ops culture or CEO's vision; ineffective communication; leaves after 18 months.

Why it fails: Search process focused on credentials, not cultural fit.

Prevention: Multiple interviews with CEO + COO; structured debrief; reference calls that include ops leadership.

8. Career Trajectory After Role

Typical Next Moves

Success trajectory:
- Ops Innovation VP → SVP Operations (next logical step; proven they understand P&L + change)
- Ops Innovation SVP → Chief Operating Officer (increasingly common pathway; CEO track)
- Ops Innovation SVP → Chief Digital Officer (if company separates digital from ops)
- Ops Innovation (external hire) → Group President / Market Leader (if multi-unit operator)

Exit patterns (concerning):
- Ops Innovation role → Consultant (person returns to consulting; ops role seen as stepping stone)
- Ops Innovation role → CIO/Tech role (person pivots toward tech; signals poor ops-tech integration)
- Ops Innovation role → Exit (person leaves company; signals poor fit or burnout)

Panda consideration: This is a defining role. The person who builds Panda's ops innovation discipline is likely CEO-track or eventual COO. Frame it that way.

9. Fractional / Consulting Bridge — Market Validation

Is the Model Working?

Yes. Evidence from QSR leaders:

Chipotle: Hired consulting team (Bain) for 9-month operations transformation engagement (2023–2024). Parallel search for permanent VP Innovation ops. Fractional model worked; consultant stayed on in advisory capacity post-hire.

Chick-fil-A: Used Chief Outsiders fractional leader for 6-month ops innovation pilot design (2024). Outcomes: 3 pilots validated; permanent director hired post-engagement.

Sweetgreen: Brought in interim COO (consultant) for 8 months during CEO transition (2024). Provided steady ops leadership while permanent hire was sourced.

Cava: Used Insideout interim VP Ops for 5 months while searching for permanent hire. Consultant stayed 2 weeks post-hire for transition.

Domino's: Internal pilot of "chief innovation officer" title; after 18 months, role structure revised. Fractional architectural consultant helped redesign before next permanent hire.

Why the Fractional Bridge Works

  1. De-risks the permanent hire: Scope is proven; success criteria are clear.
  2. Reduces time-to-value: Consultant starts immediately; doesn't require notice period or onboarding.
  3. Provides cover: Allows CEO/COO to say "we're taking this seriously" while buying time for a thorough search.
  4. Informs comp: Helps determine if the role is SVP or VP (or new tier entirely).
  5. Builds coalition: Consultant can spend time building trust with ops teams, franchisees, etc.

10. Brady's Positioning in the Fractional Ecosystem

Fit Assessment

Brady as fractional Ops Innovation lead (6–12 months):

Strengths:
- COO background (Panda understands the perspective).
- Retail ops + foodservice + analytics (credible operator).
- Change management DNA (has led P&L transformations).
- Not threatening to incoming permanent leader (positioned as architect, not competitor).
- Can design the role, scope the team, validate pilots, and hand off to permanent hire with playbook.

Positioning statement:
"Brady's engagement validates Panda's ops innovation hypothesis, designs the role and team structure, runs 2–3 priority pilots to establish ROI, and hands off a roadmap and candidate profile to the permanent leader. This de-risks the $450K+ annual hire and accelerates time-to-impact."

12. Key Data Points for the Conversation with James Ku

  1. Titling doesn't matter yet. Until Panda defines the role, the title is secondary. What matters is clarity on scope, reporting line, and success metrics.

  2. $400K–$550K base is the market rate for SVP. If Panda is thinking VP-level, budget $300K–$400K. Equity or profit-sharing can offset base if Panda wants to stay lean on salary.

  3. Retained search takes 4–6 months minimum. If James wants someone in seat by Q4 2026, the search needs to start in June 2026.

  4. The fractional bridge is increasingly standard. It's not a signal of uncertainty; it's a risk-mitigation strategy used by Chipotle, CFA, and others.

  5. Failure to embed in ops P&L is the #1 reason these hires fail. The new leader must be accountable to unit economics, not just innovation metrics.

Report Status: Complete | Word Count: 5,200 | Sources: 30+

Store Development & Format Innovation Benchmark

Date: April 15, 2026 | Focus: QSR/Fast-Casual Store Design, Kitchen Innovation, International Benchmarks

Executive Summary

The QSR industry is in the middle of the most aggressive format innovation cycle in decades. The common thread: smaller dining rooms, larger kitchens, digital-first throughput, and drive-thru reimagination. Panda Express's "Panda Home" prototype fits this trend but lags the boldest movers (Chick-fil-A's elevated drive-thru, Sweetgreen's robotic makeline, Taco Bell Defy). Internationally, China leads in kitchen automation and robotics. The CDO opportunity at Panda is to leapfrog incrementalism with data-driven format decisions and bolder physical innovation.

Section 1: Panda's Own Store Development Moves

Panda Home Prototype (May 2023)

Panda Express debuted its new "Panda Home" store design in Dripping Springs, Texas — a cultural heritage-inspired prototype that represents the company's first major format evolution.

Design language:
- Black and white color palette with red accents and wood tones
- Moon gate-inspired entrance
- Neon signs and murals celebrating Chinese-American culture
- Custom artwork by local Asian American artists (Steffi Lynn for the Texas location)
- Terrazzo tables symbolizing Chinese New Year confetti
- Traditional fretwork tiling and red lanterns
- Drive-thru lined with pictograms telling an "American Chinese story"

Operational changes:
- Back-of-house expanded — larger kitchen footprint to improve speed of service
- Dining room reduced ~15% — reflecting the industry-wide shift toward off-premise
- Digital pickup area enhanced — additional screens for order tracking as digital orders increase
- Kitchen display systems — digital screens replacing paper tickets

Rollout status:
- Debuted: Dripping Springs, TX (May 2023)
- March 2026: North Platte, NE became first city with updated restaurant design
- Rolling out to new locations in multiple US markets

Drive-Thru Buildout

  • 75% of recent new builds include drive-thru — a fundamental shift from Panda's mall-heritage
  • This is arguably the most important format decision in the company's recent history
  • Drive-thru was crucial during COVID and continues to drive traffic as dine-in hasn't fully recovered

Tea Bar Integration

  • 39 locations feature an in-restaurant Tea Bar as of 2025
  • Bubble tea, artisanal tea infusions (designed by a Taiwanese tea master), pastries
  • A concept-within-a-concept: adds a beverage daypart and incremental revenue per location
  • Physical design requires dedicated counter space and equipment within the box

Smart Building Technology (GridPoint)

  • Jan 2024: Partnership announced to deploy smart building tech across all ~2,500 US locations
  • 18-month timeline — should be substantially complete by mid-2025
  • Automates HVAC and lighting controls
  • Real-time operational and environmental performance monitoring
  • Based on 200-store pilot with measurable energy savings and CO2 reductions
  • Restaurants use 5–7x more energy per square foot than commercial buildings

Kitchen Innovation

  • Automated woks deployed for consistency and speed
  • Clamshell grills — faster-cooking equipment for protein items
  • Innovation Kitchen in Pasadena, CA — "living laboratory" for testing new menu, format, and equipment innovations
  • Kitchen innovations are tested here before system-wide rollout

Section 2: US QSR Competitive Benchmark — Format Innovation

Chick-fil-A — Elevated Drive-Thru

The boldest format innovation in US QSR.

  • Concept: Two-story building with kitchen elevated to second level, four drive-thru lanes threading underneath
  • First location: McDonough, Georgia (August 2024)
  • Capacity: Space for 75 cars. Handles 2–3x the volume of a standard drive-thru
  • Lane configuration: 2 lanes for Mobile Thru (app orders, QR check-in), 2 lanes for on-site ordering with team members
  • Kitchen: 2x larger than typical Chick-fil-A. Food conveyor delivers a meal every 6 seconds
  • No dining room — drive-thru only format
  • Same footprint as traditional freestanding, but vertical arrangement maximizes throughput
  • 2025 expansion: Deerfield Beach and University at Sunrise locations with multilane drive-throughs
  • Why it matters for Panda: Chick-fil-A is proving that vertical construction + lane multiplication can dramatically increase throughput without more land. Panda's drive-thru buildout could benefit from similar thinking.

Chipotle — Chipotlane

Digital-only drive-thru at scale.

  • Concept: Dedicated lane for mobile order pickup only — no ordering at the window
  • Transaction time: Under 30 seconds (vs. 3–5 minutes for traditional QSR drive-thru)
  • Sales lift: 10–15% increase at Chipotlane-equipped locations vs. traditional format
  • Penetration: ~80% of company-owned stores will have Chipotlane by 2026
  • Scale: ~2,000 locations with full new equipment package by end of 2026
  • Why it matters for Panda: Chipotlane proves that a dedicated digital pickup lane is a profit driver, not just a convenience. With 42% digital sales, Panda is ripe for a dedicated digital-order lane.

Taco Bell — Defy

The most radical QSR format experiment.

  • Concept: Two-story, four drive-thru lane restaurant with vertical lift system
  • First location: Suburban Minneapolis
  • Lane functions: One for app pre-orders, one for delivery drivers, one for traditional ordering, one flexible
  • Unique feature: Vertical lift system transports food from second-story kitchen to cars — contactless delivery
  • Order time: Under 2 minutes
  • Why it matters for Panda: Defy shows that QSR brands are willing to radically rethink the box. The delivery-driver dedicated lane is particularly relevant as delivery grows.

Sweetgreen — Infinite Kitchen (Robotic Makeline)

Automation as a core format strategy.

  • Concept: Robotic assembly line that builds salads/bowls automatically
  • Throughput: ~500 orders per hour
  • Performance: 7 percentage points labor savings, 1 point COGS improvement vs. traditional stores
  • Ticket lift: 10% higher average tickets at automated locations
  • Deployment: 25 new Infinite Kitchen locations in 2025, targeting all new locations within 5 years
  • Business move: Sold robotics subsidiary Spyce to Wonder for ~$186M, licensing the technology back
  • Drive-thru combo: First "Sweetlane" location combines automated makeline with drive-thru in California
  • Why it matters for Panda: Panda's steam table / wok-to-order format could benefit from automation — automated woks are already deployed, but a fully automated kitchen assembly system would be the next frontier.

McDonald's — CosMc's (Cautionary Tale)

Small-format experiment that didn't survive.

  • Concept: Small-format, beverage-led spinoff brand inspired by alien mascot
  • First location: Bolingbrook, Illinois (2023)
  • Plan: ~10 pilot locations in Dallas-Fort Worth and San Antonio metros
  • Result: All 5 CosMc's locations closed by end of June 2025 — less than 2 years
  • Pivot: Beverages inspired by CosMc's will be tested at regular McDonald's locations
  • Why it matters for Panda: Cautionary tale about spinoff formats. Panda's Tea Bar strategy (concept-within-a-concept, not standalone) may be the wiser approach. Innovation inside the existing box > standalone experiments.

Shake Shack — Drive-Thru Pilots

  • Testing drive-thru at select locations — historically a dine-in/walk-up brand
  • Smaller footprint tests in suburban markets
  • Digital ordering integration with pickup windows

Wingstop — Digital-First Small Format

  • Ghost kitchen and delivery-focused strategy
  • Small footprint, minimal dining room
  • 98%+ digital ordering penetration at some locations
  • Fastest-growing QSR chain by unit economics

Raising Cane's — Rapid Growth Standardization

  • Extremely standardized box design enables rapid buildout
  • Simple menu (chicken fingers only) = simplified kitchen design
  • One of the fastest-growing chains by new unit openings

Section 3: International QSR Innovation

China — The Global Leader in Restaurant Automation

Haidilao Smart Restaurant (Beijing)
- First "smart" restaurant opened 2018, built with Panasonic robotics
- Giant robotic arms select and prepare ingredients with perfect portion control
- Six humanoid robots serve between kitchen and dining area
- ~30% kitchen staff reduction through automation
- AI assistant "Xiaolaolao" handles reservations, table booking, group size confirmation
- March 2026: A humanoid robot server at Haidilao in Cupertino, CA went viral after "going berserk" while dancing — showing both the ambition and the growing pains of restaurant robotics

Keenon Robotics & Pudu Robotics (Chinese Startups)
- China accounted for 18%+ of global restaurant robot unit sales in 2025
- Tens of thousands of delivery/serving robots deployed in hotpot chains, hospital canteens, hotel restaurants
- These robots are now being exported globally — showing up in US and European restaurants

China's Catering Industry Tech Push
- AI-powered ingredient tracking
- Smart reservation systems
- Robotic chefs in multiple chain formats
- The Chinese restaurant industry is essentially a living laboratory for restaurant automation at scale

Japan — Conveyor Innovation & Robot Pioneers

Kura Sushi
- 600+ locations worldwide
- Pioneered conveyor-belt delivery system
- "Kurabot" robot brings drinks and condiments to tables
- April 2025: Unveiled world's longest sushi conveyor belt (135 meters) at Osaka-Kansai Expo, serving 338 diners simultaneously
- Japan has led restaurant robotics since the early 2000s, with robot-staffed eateries as cultural norm

South Korea — Labor Shortage Driving Automation

  • Aging workforce and rising labor costs accelerating kitchen automation investment
  • Substantial franchise restaurant sector adopting robotic solutions
  • Delivery robot deployment in urban areas

Europe — Sustainability-Forward Design

  • UK, Germany, France leading adoption of robotic food prep and AI customer engagement
  • Sustainability and energy regulations driving adoption of robotics for optimized operations and reduced food waste
  • Net-zero restaurant concepts emerging (LEED-certified builds, renewable energy, zero-waste kitchens)
  • Urban micro-formats for dense European city centers

Middle East — Premium QSR Formats

  • Technology-forward ordering (kiosks, app integration standard)
  • Premium QSR positioning — higher-end interiors for the same fast-food concepts
  • Panda Express already has presence in Saudi Arabia and UAE

Market Scale

  • Global restaurant robotics market: $3.8B in 2025
  • QSR segment: $1.67B (44% of total)
  • Asia-Pacific CAGR: 17.4% through 2034
  • AI + Robotics in QSR market: Expected to reach $12.91B by 2032 (11.54% CAGR)
  • North America: 40% market share

Section 4: Restaurant Design & Construction Trends

Modular / Prefab Construction

  • Speed: 30–50% faster than traditional construction
  • Cost: 20–30% less per square foot
  • Process: Up to 90% of construction completed off-site (kitchen systems, plumbing, electrical, HVAC, finishes all pre-assembled)
  • Global market: $103B in 2024, projected $162B by 2030 (7.9% CAGR)
  • QSR relevance: Enables faster pipeline velocity — critical when adding 100+ stores per year
  • Why it matters for Panda: With 123 new stores planned for 2025, modular construction could dramatically accelerate the build pipeline and reduce per-unit costs

Drive-Thru Design Evolution

  • Dual/multi-lane becoming standard for high-volume locations
  • Dedicated digital lanes (Chipotlane model) separating mobile orders from traditional ordering
  • Delivery driver lanes (Taco Bell Defy) — dedicated infrastructure for third-party delivery
  • Vertical/elevated concepts (Chick-fil-A) — going up when you can't go out
  • Pickup windows vs. cubbies — some brands testing order-ahead lockers instead of windows

Smaller Footprints

  • Industry-wide trend: reduce dining room, expand kitchen/BOH
  • Panda's approach: 15% smaller dining room in Panda Home prototype
  • Others going further: Chick-fil-A's elevated concept has zero dining room
  • Ghost kitchens: Delivery-only facilities with no customer-facing space (Wingstop, many virtual brands)
  • The math: Less dining room = lower rent, fewer front-of-house staff, more kitchen capacity for digital orders

Kitchen Automation Equipment

  • Robotic fryers: Deployed at multiple chains (Flippy by Miso Robotics at Jack in the Box, White Castle, others)
  • Automated assembly: Sweetgreen's Infinite Kitchen is the leader
  • Smart ventilation: AI-controlled ventilation systems that adjust to cooking activity
  • Automated woks: Already at Panda (competitive advantage in Asian QSR — the wok is harder to automate than a fryer)
  • Kitchen display systems: Replacing paper tickets across the industry. Panda rolling out.

AI Applications in Store Development

  • Site selection: AI models predicting new store performance based on demographics, traffic, competition, weather patterns
  • Trade area analysis: Machine learning for defining optimal trade areas and cannibalization avoidance
  • Layout optimization: Using throughput data to optimize kitchen flow and equipment placement
  • Energy management: GridPoint-style smart building platforms (Panda is already doing this)
  • Predictive maintenance: Anticipating equipment failures before they cause downtime
  • Construction project management: AI-powered scheduling, cost tracking, contractor management

Sustainability in Restaurant Construction

  • Net-zero builds emerging (LEED-certified restaurants)
  • Energy efficiency: Smart building tech (GridPoint), solar panels, LED lighting, high-efficiency HVAC
  • Materials innovation: Sustainable materials, reduced concrete/steel, timber construction
  • Water efficiency: Low-flow fixtures, greywater recycling
  • Panda's position: GridPoint deployment puts them ahead of most QSR chains on energy management. Opportunity to go further with net-zero prototypes.

Key Takeaways for the James Ku Conversation

  1. Panda is doing the right things but incrementally. Panda Home is a solid evolution, but competitors are making bolder bets (Chick-fil-A elevated, Sweetgreen robotics, Taco Bell Defy).

  2. The drive-thru buildout is the most important strategic move. Transitioning from mall-centric to drive-thru freestanding is existentially important. 75% of new builds with drive-thru is good — the question is whether the drive-thru format itself is bold enough.

  3. Digital sales at 42% demand a physical response. When nearly half your revenue comes through digital channels, the box needs to be designed around digital fulfillment, not retrofitted for it. Dedicated digital pickup lanes (Chipotlane model) should be on the table.

  4. Kitchen automation is the next frontier. Automated woks are a start, but the Sweetgreen Infinite Kitchen model shows that full makeline automation delivers real margin improvement (7 pts labor, 1 pt COGS). Panda's wok format is harder to automate than salad assembly, but not impossible.

  5. International innovation is moving faster. China's restaurant robotics (Haidilao, Keenon, Pudu) are 3–5 years ahead of US deployment. James's Walmart China experience makes him the right person to bridge this gap.

  6. Modular construction could unlock pipeline velocity. At 123 new stores per year, even a 30% time reduction per build is significant.

  7. AI in site selection is underexploited. Most chains still use traditional real estate methods. AI-powered site selection is an enormous opportunity for a chain building 100+ stores annually.
